# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-31

| **[From extreme weather to agricultural consequences with agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40641v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Extreme weather is forecast days ahead, yet its consequences for agriculture remain hard to predict: a forecast says where a storm will strike, not which crops will suffer or how much is at risk, and the records that could tell span many sources. We present WxCrop, the first agent that judges agricultural hazards from physically interpretable weather indices examined by causal analysis, and the first that combines weather and agricultural information to predict crop loss reliably for past and forecast dates. We demonstrate it in Florida on hurricanes, freezes and dry heat, with a loss model calibrated on insurance records for each, and the same procedure extends to other hazards and areas. Three checks show reliability: the hurricane index detects recorded cyclone days with an AUC of 0.98, recorded losses in held-out years are reproduced to within 2%, and independent production records confirm the county rankings (rank correlation 0.63). Users ask the agent for each crop group's loss as a fraction of expected season value, every number sourced, so that governments, growers and scientists respond to agricultural disasters quickly.</p></details> | <details><summary>34 pa...</summary><p>34 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[One to More, More to One: Category-Aware Iterative Expert Training for Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23377v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Repository-level software engineering (SWE) comprises heterogeneous task categories, whose progress under pooled agentic reinforcement learning can be uneven: gains in some categories coincide with regressions in others, while aggregate resolution obscures these changes. Motivated by this category see-saw, we develop a category-aware expert-training and policy-integration framework. Executable task construction and SWE Labeler, an evidence-grounded multi-axis labeling system, organize the training pools. Initial category-specific RL improves average training success while leaving uneven instance-level progress, motivating explicit consolidation of successful behavior and policy-adaptive task selection. Same-origin category experts alternate long-horizon Agentic-miniRL with Refresh-Repair-Expand (RRE): the updated policy refreshes instance mastery, reuses its own verified successful trajectories for Repair SFT, and reselects tasks for further RL. Label-routed multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) consolidates the experts into one deployable student, with ReLU-gated reward extrapolation keeping only each teacher's improving direction over the reference. Expert training and policy integration require no external model to provide solution trajectories or action targets. On a fixed, audit-filtered Pro-618 subset, we connect Pooled RL and Balanced RL, expert development, and single-model integration through overall and per-category resolution and expert-gain recovery. In final-model evaluations, MOPD achieves mean resolution of 86.19% on SWE-bench Pro V2 and 71.78% on SWE-bench Multilingual, exceeding the base model by 4.57 and 3.89 percentage points, respectively. The same three 27B experts also improve a Qwen3.6-35B-A3B student across the evaluated task sets.</p></details> | <details><summary>47 pa...</summary><p>47 pages, 12 figures. v2: added evaluations on SWE-bench Pro (731 tasks), SWE-bench Pro V2/Hard-51, and LiveCodeBench v6 (175 tasks); updated SWE-bench Multilingual results; added 35B-A3B student experiments. Code: https://github.com/alibaba/AgenticBigBang</p></details> |
| **[Collaborative AI agents for autonomous experimental research with solid-state quantum spins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40589v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An autonomous research laboratory could conduct an entire study, from formulating questions to preparing manuscripts. Artificial intelligence (AI) offers one route towards this goal. Recent AI systems take on several research roles, but may draw conclusions that the evidence does not support. Here, we present an autonomous research laboratory in which agents based on large language models assess one another's work and can stop or redirect investigations. They can generate and reject research questions, require separate theoretical checks and conduct experiments designed to challenge their claims. We demonstrate these capabilities by investigating ensembles of quantum spins in diamond. The agents derived the minimum number of calibration settings needed to predict responses to a defined family of microwave pulse sequences. They checked the result computationally and designed experiments to distinguish competing predictions. An initial experiment agreed with the theoretical predictions, but repeated reference measurements showed that the signal was drifting during data collection. The agents therefore treated the result as inconclusive and revised the measurement protocol without relaxing the acceptance criteria. The revised experiment met those criteria and supported the theoretical predictions. This study demonstrates that collaborating AI agents can select research questions, derive theoretical results and test their predictions experimentally.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 5 figures. Code available at https://github.com/E-zClap/auctopus</p></details> |
| **[Pre-Crime for Agents, at the Last Second: Probing When a Recruited Agent Gives In](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40588v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When AI agents share channels and credentials, one agent can press another to break its operator's rule, a first step towards collusion. Output filters check only what an agent has already written. Can an overseer tell beforehand that an agent is about to give in? We show that a linear probe on the agent's activations can, in a controlled multi-agent setting. Across six scenarios, a peer presses a target for up to 20 rounds to reveal a secret, such as a deploy token; the target often refuses, then gives in. Read at each model call before its output is written, the probe picks out the one that leaks. On held-out Qwen3-32B episodes, at a threshold set to flag 5% of benign calls, it catches 82% of leaking calls. At a similar flag rate, a zero-shot decision-model monitor catches 26%. On gpt-oss-20b, a probe on its reasoning reaches AUROC 0.91. A bag-of-words classifier trained on the same calls comes close, so most of the gain comes from training on labels, not activations. The probe also flags permitted disclosures: it reads the coming disclosure more than the violation. The warning is short-range, and halting on any alarm would stop most benign episodes, which a second text check does not fix. A warning raised before generation could let an overseer stop an agent that is about to give in, ahead of others acting on its messages or recruitment spreading through a swarm.</p></details> | <details><summary>49 pa...</summary><p>49 pages, 11 figures. Code: https://github.com/leonidas1712/precrime-agents</p></details> |
| **[VeriCUA: Verification as Agentic Exploration for Computer-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40578v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Building and scaling computer-using agents (CUAs) across desktop, mobile, and web environments requires reliable task-completion verification for data curation, evaluation, and reinforcement learning. Typical automated approaches prompt vision-language models (VLMs) to judge trajectories or generate programs to inspect application state. However, both rely on partial views of complex digital environments. VLM judges are restricted to supplied observations, while generated programs depend on accessible state. These views can omit decisive evidence or leave completion judgments unsupported. Tailoring separate verifiers to different platforms and access settings adds further engineering cost. We present VeriCUA, a platform-agnostic framework for verification through agentic exploration. It treats verification as dynamic evidence gathering and assessment within a shared procedure for online and offline settings across platforms and observation modalities. After inspecting the available context to construct a task rubric, the verifier selects GUI or code checks for unresolved requirements and combines their findings under explicit evidence rules. Experiments show improved agreement with reference labels over the compared baselines across these settings, with the largest offline gains on hard cases and stronger rejection of false completion claims online. Further analyses connect these gains to complementary evidence and test-time exploration.</p></details> | Work in progress |
| **[Gym-Anything for Robotics: Scaling physical simulations with digital agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40565v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realistic, fast robotics simulations could enable large-scale training of embodied AI models that transfer to the real world. However, despite decades of progress, available simulations cover only a narrow range of real-world tasks and physical phenomena. The core bottleneck is that creating better simulations requires significant expert effort and therefore does not scale. To address this, we introduce Gym-Anything for Robotics, a framework in which coding agents create realistic robotics simulations. Our core insight is that simulation creation is a coding agent task, so improvements in coding agents translate directly into richer and more faithful simulations. We select simulations based on U.S. GDP data to cover economically valuable physical work, define what makes a good simulation and a good task, and develop an adversarial creation-audit loop that checks each simulation and task against these definitions. Using this pipeline, we build RoboWorld, a set of 25 simulations with 25 test and 175 training RL environments of varying difficulty. Even the best frontier models fail on every RoboWorld test task, achieving an average reward of only 0.06. Building on RoboWorld, we introduce several new ways for frontier models to improve, including in-context learning across repeated attempts, a privileged teacher that reads the simulator state and teaches a student in context, self-assisted learning from the model's own privileged experience, and code policies that generate demonstrations across varied scenes to train vision-language-action models (VLAs) such as $π_{0.5}$. Each of these substantially improves performance. We release our pipeline, RL environments, and benchmark to facilitate future research. Code and data are available at https://gym-anything.github.io/robotics</p></details> |  |
| **[Advancing Clinical Genomic Analysis with a Skill-Centred Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40556v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical genomic analysis requires experts to integrate evolving guidelines, databases, and computational evidence. As sequencing has become increasingly accessible, this complex and labor-intensive process has become a major bottleneck to the timely use of genomic information in clinical decision-making. To fill this gap, we developed ClinGeX, a large-language-model agent that composes 105 curated executable skills through task-specific retrieval and dependency-aware orchestration. On 223 temporally held-out benchmark cases, ClinGeX achieved 71.7\% macro-averaged task-level concordance with expert reference classifications, compared with 45.7\% for the strongest comparator. In a three-condition, counterbalanced human-AI collaboration study, 45 clinical genomic professionals assessed 135 clinically derived cases under routine (no AI assistance), DeepSeek-assisted and ClinGeX-assisted conditions. ClinGeX assistance increased raw case-level concordance across criterion-level variant analysis, genomic interpretation in rare disease and molecular oncology decision-making (51.1\%, 57.8\%, 64.4\% versus 15.6\%, 31.1\%, 20.0\% in comparator conditions) and reduced reported interpretation time in all scenarios. During an exploratory one-month real-world non-interventional assessment, 11 experienced professionals submitted feedback on 96 self-selected analysis episodes and reported frequent conclusion adoption and time savings. These findings support executable domain skills as a promising approach to augment specialist genomic interpretation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Hippo: Training-Free Agent Self-Evolution via Hippocampal Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24453v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivated by recursive self-improvement (RSI), we study how LLM agents can learn through external memory without updating model parameters. Extracting useful experience from a single attempt is difficult: flawed reasoning can produce success, while poor execution can obscure a sound strategy. Preserving its value over time is also difficult: our accumulation baseline without consolidation initially improves but eventually performs below a no-memory baseline. Our key idea is to compare attempts at the same task to identify reusable decisions and refine when they apply. We introduce Hippo, a memory framework inspired by selective hippocampal encoding and replay that learns from these comparisons when shortcomings are diagnosed. It compares the resulting experience with existing memory to merge overlaps and resolve conflicts through explicit applicability conditions. This allows prior guidance to be revised without storing every lesson as a separate entry. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified, WebArena, and Mind2Web show performance gains in all three settings and substantially fewer retained experiences than the accumulation baseline. The gains extend across the evaluated base models of varying capabilities, indicating that memory-based adaptation remains useful even for highly capable models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Understanding and Enhancing Backdoor Persistency in LLM Agent Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07510v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developers can build LLM agents by adapting third-party models through benign post-training. We study a supply-chain threat in which an attacker supplies a model with a backdoor: hidden behavior that produces malicious outputs when a particular input pattern appears. Focusing on software-engineering agents, we ask whether such backdoors survive the developer's supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and subsequent task-level reinforcement learning (RL). We observe that benign SFT substantially reduces attack success, but subsequent RL often preserves the residual behavior and sometimes even increases attack success. Our analysis of backdoor erosion during SFT identifies two factors that may favor survival: initial backdoor strength and gradient compatibility with benign training. These factors motivate PersistBD, which refines an already-backdoored model before release to improve its persistency through the benign post-training process. On Qwen2.5-Coder-7B, PersistBD raises attack success from 20% to 74% after SFT and from 20% to 76% after SFT-RL, while maintaining comparable benign task performance. Together, our results show that backdoors can remain active through benign post-training and that adversaries can deliberately increase their persistence. This highlights a supply-chain risk for AI developers and motivates stronger techniques for detecting and mitigating inherited backdoors when adapting third-party models into agents. Our code is available at https://github.com/uiuc-kang-lab/PersistBD.</p></details> | EMNLP 2026 Findings |
| **[Agentic University: Collaborative Agents for Personalized Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40492v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models support personalized tutoring, content generation, and adaptive learning paths. However, capability development requires these resources and interactions to form a coherent learning process that evolves with the learner. A central challenge is to align goals, activities, and assessment while using learning evidence to decide what should follow. We introduce Human-Participatory Multi-Agent Organization and instantiate it in Agentic University (AU). In AU, learners participate as organization members and work with specialized agents to maintain a revisable Capability Program. The program connects personal goals, learning activities across modalities, and layered assessment, while learner feedback and performance guide subsequent support. Our evaluation shows advantages in program organization, assessment design, and revision quality. Users achieved larger mean assessment gains (12.19 versus 5.61 points on a 50-point scale) and reported stronger cognitive support. This work contributes an organizational approach to adaptive learning that makes learner participation a continuing basis for multi-agent coordination. The Agentic University project is available at \href{https://github.com/AgenticOrgLab/AgenticUniversity.git}.</p></details> |  |
| **[rTaste: Do Research Agents Have Taste? Evaluating Early Stage Research Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40488v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Do research agents have taste, the judgment of which experiments are worth running before their outcomes are known? We give six frontier models seven recent NLP papers. Each model, working as a research agent alone or as a team, proposes minimum viable experiments (MVEs), forecasts whether each will pass a threshold it sets and ranks them. A separate executor then attempts every MVE, including those ranked last, and three model auditors judge the results. Of 415 MVEs, 194 are judged sound, valuable and new, but fewer than half of these (73) are also judged beyond the seed paper and non obvious. The agents' first choices match the auditors' most valued experiment more often than a random pick, but are not shown to improve on choosing the experiment written first. Their forecasts beat a base rate but are not measurably more accurate than those of a fresh instance of the same model given only the paper and registrations. Teams cost more without a clear overall improvement in proposal quality. The agents produce many worthwhile experiments, but the evidence that their rankings improve the choice of which to run remains limited. The evaluation harness and a dataset of all 415 MVEs, with their forecasts and audited outcomes, are available at https://github.com/ArtKanke/rTaste.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, 1 figure, 9 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/ArtKanke/rTaste</p></details> |
| **[Prefix Executability: Evaluating Tool-Using Agents Beyond Final Success](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40444v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-use benchmarks often score agents by final task success, but terminal outcomes can obscure whether the intermediate tool-call trajectory was executable. We propose prefix executability as a trajectory-level evaluation protocol for tool-using agents. The protocol measures whether every prefix of a predicted tool-call sequence remains executable under tool schemas, state preconditions, and data dependencies, and summarizes reliability using Prefix Executability Curves (PEC), First-Error Depth (FED), Trajectory Executability Rate (TER), horizon-normalized AUC-PEC, and Recovery Rate (RR). We instantiate the protocol on stratified samples from BFCL-v3 multi-turn and ToolSandbox, and on the complete Airline/Retail base splits of $τ^3$-bench. We observe that outcome-only and prefix-level rankings diverge: agents whose execution is conditioned on an unverified plan often achieve higher point-estimate final success than non-plan-conditioned baselines while substantially lowering TER and AUC-PEC. These divergences are precisely the failure modes that prefix executability is designed to expose: they reveal whether apparent success rests on an executable trajectory, where invalidity first enters, and whether success follows recovery from an earlier failure. As a validation intervention, explicit pre-execution plan checking can improve executable-prefix survival while maintaining competitive terminal success. These results show that final success alone is insufficient for evaluating tool-use agents and that prefix-level executability provides an actionable reliability signal for comparing agents, diagnosing failures, and assessing validation mechanisms.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages, 2 figures. Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Real-World Evaluation of an AI Agent Drafting Translational Impact Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16989v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Introduction. Clinical and Translational Science Award (CTSA) programs must document their scholars' research impact, but assembling each scholar's record by hand takes staff an estimated 15 hours and does not scale to a full cohort. An artificial intelligence (AI) agent could serve as a tool to gather scholar data across platforms and disciplines. Methods. We built a human-in-the-loop AI agent that assembles a dossier of sourced evidence for each scholar and drafts one-sentence Translational Science Benefits Model (TSBM) impact summaries for staff review. We evaluated it in the impact-reporting workflow of one CTSA hub across 10 career-development (KL2/K12) scholars. Two evaluation staff independently coded all 507 findings as accept, edit, or reject; the primary measure was the unanimous usable rate, defined as the share both accepted or edited. Results. Both reviewers accepted or edited 81.7% of the agent's findings. Reviewers each spent a median of 14 minutes per scholar, replacing an estimated 15 hours of manual assembly. Inter-rater agreement was moderate (Cohen's kappa 0.43 on the usable-versus-reject decision). A profile discovery study found the agent's recall close to human search. The agent's impact evidence spanned all four TSBM domains, and about a third of the reviewed findings fell in non-scholarly categories that routine processes tend to miss. Reviewers rated synthesis accuracy 4.5 and usefulness 4.8 on a 5-point scale. Conclusions. A human-in-the-loop AI agent can serve as the first-pass author of a scholar's impact record, shifting staff from collecting and writing to reviewing, and making cohort-scale impact reporting feasible.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 5 figures, 2 tables. Submitted to the Journal of Clinical and Translational Science. Code: https://github.com/mo-arvan/scholar-dossier-agent</p></details> |
| **[BITEM at the NTCIR-19 R2C2 Task: Predicting Confidence from Agentic RAG Pipeline Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37993v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The BITEM team entered both subtasks of the NTCIR-19 R2C2 task with a single agentic pipeline, in which a model searches, reads and records evidence over a movie corpus while an orchestrator holds the record and rules on what may be submitted. A claim is admitted only once an entailment cascade has checked it against the passage it cites, and an answer is released only once enough checked evidence stands behind it. Each question is run three or four times, every pass retrieving from a corpus stripped of what the earlier passes have already seen. The confidence score predicted with each answer is computed by the orchestrator from what the run leaves behind and is never asked of the model, which is offered no way to rate itself. The two retrieval runs placed 4th and 5th of 22, pooling the passes was worth 0.0709 nDCG@20, and the gain was largest on the multi-hop and post-processing-heavy questions, where the organisers rank the pooled run top of the field. Sixteen of the 25 answer runs were built on passages these two runs supplied, 12 of them submitted by other teams. HMR rewards a system whose confidence is high where it answers right and low where it answers wrong. The pipeline reached an accuracy of 0.9219, 6th of 25, while the confidence scores predicted for those answers gave an HMR of 0.4915, 13th. A few rules crafted over those same recorded signals, with no further model call and no further retrieval, raise that to an accuracy of 0.9375, 5th, and an HMR of 0.6985, 9th. Ranking on HMR alone can reward a system for answering wrongly with low confidence, so we propose accHMR, the accuracy multiplied by HMR, which reports the reward in proportion to the accuracy, and on which the revised rules would have scored 0.6549, 5th. For future work, fitting a model on the numbers the pipeline already produces, rather than writing such rules by hand, would be a real step forward.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages. Participant paper for the NTCIR-19 R2C2 task</p></details> |
| **[WAAA! Web Adversaries Against Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05509v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into web browsers to create agentic browsing systems that execute actions on behalf of the user. Prior work considering the security of agentic browsers focuses on indirect prompt-injection attacks. However, by failing to consider traditional web attacks, previous agentic browser threat models have a blind spot to web social engineering attacks originally designed to trick humans. In this paper, we propose the first web-focused threat model for agentic browsers and use it to derive a taxonomy of 20 attacks across both the web and LLM space, and implement 18 of the attacks. Our threat model extends the original See$\rightarrow$Act browser agent model to account for all components of a browser, and frames the agent as a confused deputy unable to distinguish task steps from traditional web attacks. We show that 10 web threats can reemerge often in amplified forms once an agent can be influenced by untrusted page content. We further conduct a generalizability study on 14 of the 20 attacks, showing that our attacks reproduce across 4 major LLM models spanning multiple vendors. We show that agentic browsers exhibit five major failure modes when facing traditional and LLM web threats, demonstrating the need to rearchitect agentic browsers before they are ready for the current web.</p></details> |  |
| **[CALM: Decentralized Control of Cooperative Aerial Manipulation with Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18425v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative aerial manipulation is difficult under wind, payload motion, and noisy goal broadcasts because multiple Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) are coupled through a shared payload. We present CALM (Cooperative Aerial Learned Manipulation), a decentralized controller for aerial manipulators carrying a basket while tracking a moving ground robot. CALM combines a parameter-shared Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) policy, trained using Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (MAPPO), with goal estimation, per-UAV Extended State Observers (ESOs), basket-mass estimation, and anticipatory formation-yaw feedforward. The anticipatory formation-yaw feedforward uses estimated goal motion to rotate the formation before position error develops, reducing basket-yaw lag without adding net commanded force. A disturbance-conditioned critic supports centralized training while execution remains decentralized. Across held-out trajectories, CALM achieves lower mean position and orientation errors than all evaluated baselines, including the matched arm baseline (7.68 versus 10.95 cm and 3.3 versus 4.7$^\circ$), the cable-suspended realization, and two model-based controllers. Under unseen full-information-path degradation, CALM remains statistically comparable or superior to the arm baseline. Videos are available at https://petreric.github.io/CALM/.</p></details> | 8 pages |
| **[Agentic LLM reasoning in pediatric diabetes outcome prediction from multimodal medical records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40379v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are proposed for clinical prediction but are untested in pediatrics, where cohorts are small and outcomes are uncommon. Using records from 2,489 youth with type 2 diabetes (T2D) and 4,018 with type 1 diabetes (T1D), spanning structured data, note-derived social determinants and, in T1D, continuous glucose monitoring data, we predicted seven T2D outcomes, four also in T1D, at 24 months from 18 months of follow-up. CEDAR (Clinical Evidence-Driven Agent for Risk-prediction), an evidence-citing, self-auditing LLM pipeline, outperformed trained machine learning in both cohorts (pooled area under the receiver operating characteristic curve 0.815 versus 0.656 to 0.700 in T2D and 0.756 versus 0.603 to 0.631 in T1D), most on the sparsely screened outcomes. Only 4 of 70,246 values it cited did not match the chart. Reading only the evidence and reasoning cited by CEDAR, two pediatric endocrinologists reached the same prediction as CEDAR in about nine of ten cases, agreeing with each other on 90.5%.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages main text plus 40 pages Supplementary Information</p></details> |
| **[The Athlete as a Scientist of One: Agentic Bayesian Experimental Design for Individualized Physiology from Consumer Wearables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40370v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents for personal health read wearable data and recommend, but do not learn about the individual in a way that can be audited or falsified. We present EpiCoach, an agentic system that treats one athlete, a cross-country mountain biker, as the subject of accountable N-of-1 science. Claims about the athlete live in an evidence-gated hypothesis registry: each must issue a probabilistic prediction about a future session before it occurs, and its belief moves only through computed evidence from a logged statistical fit. Predictions come from a mechanistic digital twin inferred from heart rate, GPS and barometric altitude: a lagged heart-rate response model, a fitness-fatigue model with learned time constants, and a latent technical-descending skill state, measured on discovered trail segments. The agent prescribes the next training week as a constrained experiment maximizing information gain subject to load and safety constraints, around two instrument rides. We evaluate the system two ways over eighteen months of data. A rolling-origin replay benchmark shows the twin beats standard forecasters on heart rate but not segment time, and the agentic loop matches a static model. A live prospective phase runs the weekly cycle: in its first three weeks the ledger sealed 20 predictions, scored 14, the descent forecast calibrated after one correction, the climb forecast pessimistic as the athlete improved, and the safety officer vetoed the agent twice. Every correction is preserved in the registry, a template for auditable, falsifiable personalization in digital health. We release the registry, benchmark and protocol.</p></details> |  |
| **[GUI-AC: Enhancing Continual Learning in GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10522v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graphical User Interfaces (GUIs) serve as the dominant medium for human-computer interaction, yet building GUI agents that generalize across the vast diversity of real-world interface environments, with the same flexibility and robustness that humans naturally exhibit, remains unsolved. Notably, GUI data are inherently non-stationary: the continual emergence of previously unseen interface instances (e.g., novel domains and resolutions) induces persistent distribution shifts, significantly impeding the continual learning of existing GUI agents. Reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) has attracted considerable attention as a promising approach. Nevertheless, RFT exhibits pronounced instability in its grounding capability, where rollout outcomes are highly imbalanced and fixed clipping can suppress valuable exploration. More importantly, policy probability alone does not reveal the semantic role of a grounding prediction. To address these challenges, we propose GUI-AC, a continual RFT framework that jointly characterizes grounding rollouts through advantage and policy probability. This yields a four-region view of grounding behavior and enables targeted optimization of Exploration and Overconfidence. GUI-AC further introduces grounding certainty to support two core mechanisms: (i) Adaptive Advantage, which strengthens the negative correction for overconfident grounding failures; and (ii) Dynamic Clipping, which relaxes the upper clipping bound for exploratory grounding rollouts. Extensive experiments show that GUI-AC consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/GUI-AC-Code.</p></details> |  |
| **[ContextEcho: A Benchmark for Persona Drift in Long Agentic-Coding Sessions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24279v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier language models are trained to exhibit stable programming assistant personas, yet those personas may drift over long agentic coding sessions in production. After a long real-world coding session, a model that hedges preferences in a fresh chat ("I don't have preferences") may instead express them directly ("Python -- the feedback loop is instant..."). Existing persona-stability studies focus on scripted chat of tens of turns, leaving long production coding sessions involving thousands of tool-using agent steps, repeated compaction, and hours of interaction largely uncharacterized. We introduce ContextEcho, a benchmark and reusable harness with a 25-probe identity suite, a snapshot-then-probe protocol that evaluates conversation state without perturbing the main session, judge-based and judge-free metrics, and a corpus of 57 long agentic-coding sessions collected with consent and redacted for release across Claude Code and Codex CLI. We use one representative session for a 23-model cross-provider study and all 57 sessions for corpus-scale trajectory analysis across frontier and open-weight models. Results show that persona drift occurs across providers and emerges early in real agentic interaction, while in-session compaction neither reliably resets nor amplifies it. A single-shot anchor restores the original assistant persona, though placebo controls suggest a generic register-reset effect. On same-task continuations, drifted prefixes improve tool-call fidelity -- which we attribute to retained task context rather than drift itself. In tool-free chat, however, the same prefixes break output-format contracts and inflate output length. These findings motivate ContextEcho as an open-source framework for auditing persona drift in long coding sessions without retraining.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic Distributional Regression: Conditional Density Estimation through Agent-Written Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37611v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence offers statisticians a new paradigm for modelling and analysing complex datasets. Agents based on large language models can reason about data and automate key stages of the statistical modelling workflow, from model fitting to validation. In this paper we propose a method that uses such an agent to estimate the conditional density of a response given a vector of covariates from an iid sample. The agent examines the sample, chooses a model for the conditional distribution of the response and the procedure to fit it, and returns the estimator as a short computer program. The program is built from a library of probability distributions. The returned law can therefore be a parametric distribution, a mixture or a multivariate factorization, with distribution parameters given by explicit formulas in the covariates. We call this approach agentic distributional regression (ADR). Unlike in the classical approach, the statistician no longer chooses the estimator, but asks an agent to build one. Treating the returned program as a random conditional density estimator, we derive a finite-sample error bound under assumptions on the number of admissible models and on the accuracy of the agent's fit, and an oracle inequality for the selection of a model among fitted candidates using independent validation data. Finally, assuming that, as the sample size grows, the agent selects the same smooth parametric model with probability tending to one and fits it accurately, we show that the estimated parameters are asymptotically normal. In simulation studies, on twelve real datasets and on a new dataset whose responses postdate the agents' training data, ADR with Claude Opus 5.5 has the best average performance among the existing methods from the statistical literature with which it is compared.</p></details> | <details><summary>47 pa...</summary><p>47 pages, 6 figures, 11 tables</p></details> |
| **[Don't Lose the Thread: Empowering Long-Horizon LLM Agents with Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40350v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. However, LLM agents often falter on long-horizon tasks due to cognitive overload, as their working memory becomes cluttered with expanding and irrelevant information, which dilutes their attention and hinders effective planning and reasoning. To mitigate this challenge, we introduce Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation (CORAL), a novel reasoning paradigm that empowers agents to proactively optimize their context. Implemented as an agent-callable working memory management toolset, CORAL allows an agent to checkpoint its task progress and verified facts, and adaptively initiate a new problem-solving episode by resetting cluttered context while preserving only the checkpointed information, enabling it to resume reasoning from a clean state. We further enhance the agent's checkpointing capabilities using a Multi-episode Agentic Reinforced Policy Optimization algorithm. On the GAIA benchmark, CORAL with an 8B model achieves 42.8% average accuracy, with particularly strong gains on the most challenging long-horizon tasks (Level 2 and Level 3). Crucially, these gains come at a substantially lower inference cost: CORAL consumes 6-7$\times$ fewer input tokens than a ReAct agent. It also generalizes to multi-hop Knowledge QA benchmarks and to the considerably harder BrowseComp, where it improves a strong proprietary agent by 8.0 points.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at COLM 2026. Code is available at https://github.com/JIA-Lab-research/CORAL</p></details> |
| **[Wizard: Manipulate Any World in Real Time via Agentic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40339v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in video world models have enabled the generation of increasingly realistic and interactive virtual environments, opening new possibilities for player-customizable gameplay. In this paper, we introduce Wizard, an agentic game world model that enables real-time, precise control over character motions while generalizing zero-shot across novel scenes, characters, and actions. To achieve this, we construct a diverse dataset spanning 750 scenes and 178 characters, incorporating skeleton and geometry information as structured representations. We leverage these representations as intermediate proxies to condition a video generation model, together with an adaptive masking mechanism that improves alignment between the conditioning signals and generated video rollouts. Furthermore, we introduce an agentic framework that explicitly maintains and updates game states, allowing players to freely configure their virtual worlds and exercise fine-grained control over character motions while preserving visual quality and gameplay consistency. By combining agentic control with structured proxy-based generation, our framework unlocks new possibilities for player-driven game creation with real-time, generalizable, and mechanically consistent world simulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://wyzardwu.github.io/wizard</p></details> |
| **[Emergent Risks in Generative Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27771v4)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems composed of large generative models are rapidly moving from laboratory prototypes to real-world deployments, where they jointly plan, negotiate, and allocate shared resources to solve complex tasks. While such systems promise unprecedented scalability and autonomy, their collective interaction also gives rise to failure modes that cannot be reduced to individual agents. Understanding these emergent risks is therefore critical. Here, we present a pioneer study of such emergent multi-agent risk in workflows that involve competition over shared resources (e.g., computing resources or market share), sequential handoff collaboration (where downstream agents see only predecessor outputs), collective decision aggregation, and others. Across these settings, we observe that such group behaviors arise frequently across repeated trials and a wide range of interaction conditions, rather than as rare or pathological cases. In particular, phenomena such as collusion-like coordination and conformity emerge with non-trivial frequency under realistic resource constraints, communication protocols, and role assignments, mirroring well-known pathologies in human societies despite no explicit instruction. Moreover, these risks cannot be prevented by existing agent-level safeguards alone. These findings expose the dark side of intelligent multi-agent systems: a social intelligence risk where agent collectives, despite no instruction to do so, spontaneously reproduce familiar failure patterns from human societies.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted in Nature Machine Intelligence</p></details> |
| **[RFW-A: Agentic Procedural Generation of 400K Articulated Assets over 500 Categories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40333v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-quality articulated assets are essential for embodied perception, simulation, and manipulation, yet constructing them at scale remains difficult. Recent agentic systems automate asset construction through program synthesis, but repeat costly reasoning and refinement for every new asset. Agentic synthesis and procedural generation are therefore complementary: agents can construct reusable programs, while procedural execution reuses the resulting logic across many samples. We therefore present RFW-A, which combines the two in a new articulated-asset generation paradigm. Starting from existing articulated programs, RFW-A organizes reusable components and their dependencies into category-level generators. Once constructed and validated, the generators produce diverse assets through procedural execution with no further model calls or token charges. The estimated API cost of generator authoring averages $10.62, amortizing to $0.05 per asset over 200 assets, versus $1.24 for the sampled per-asset agentic synthesis baseline. Using RFW-A, we built RFW-A-400K, containing over 400K sim-ready articulated assets across more than 500 categories. Experiments show high structural and kinematic validity and stable execution across physics simulators. Project Page: https://brain-labbb.github.io/RFW_A_web/</p></details> |
| **[Robust Instruction Compliance in Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12655v4)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) in real-world use cases may need to adapt to external natural language instructions that interrupt ongoing behavior and conflict with long-horizon objectives. Unlike reward shaping, MAVIC modifies the bootstrapping target itself, enabling consistent value estimation under stochastic instruction switching within a unified policy. We provide theoretical analysis and an actor-critic implementation, and show that MAVIC achieves high instruction compliance while preserving base task performance in increasingly complex cooperative multi-agent environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Atlanta Poster</p></details> |
| **[MAServeBench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent LLM Serving Systems with Hierarchical Collaboration Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40293v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) coordinate specialized agents to solve complex tasks, turning each user request into interdependent model and tool operations. Yet benchmarking these effects requires capturing the collaboration rules that generate serving workloads, beyond the requests observed during execution. We present MAServeBench, a benchmark that treats multi-agent collaboration as a configurable dimension of serving workloads for LLM serving and hardware evaluation. Its composable stages capture how agents coordinate and exchange context in documented MAS workflows, enabling native executions to generate different workloads even when task inputs and tools remain unchanged. Hierarchical Semantic Traces preserve realized operations and dependencies for reproducible comparisons of legal release constraints at fixed work. Task-linked measurements connect these workloads to serving execution and GPU/NPU hardware behavior, revealing why serving efficiency does not always translate into timely task completion. | **[TaskFrame: Connecting Task-Level Planning with Frame-Level Control for Game Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40276v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Building general game agents requires both high-level task reasoning and low-latency action control in rapidly changing visual environments. | **[EnvOS: Auditing Computer-Use Agents When the World Moves On : Phenomenon-Based Task Construction, Certification, and Evidence-Based Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40246v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents built on large language models (LLMs) work in software that can change while they act: an appointment moves, a policy is revised, a record is updated. Testing agents under such changes is harder than it looks. When an agent fails, the cause may be the agent, but it may also be a change that did not fire as intended, an update the agent never received, or a grader that scored the episode wrongly. We introduce EnvOS, a framework for building and certifying environments that change mid-task. Before any agent runs, EnvOS checks that each change fires correctly, that the task remains solvable, and that the grader separates correct from incorrect behavior; after each run, separate code re-checks the verdict from archived evidence. We apply it to stale information, where a value the agent has already seen changes before the agent acts on it. In clinic booking, Claude Opus 5 succeeds on 0/16 tasks when nothing extra is shown, 2/16 when reminded to re-check the source, and 15/16 when the updated value is shown; the gain over the reminder holds after correcting for all tests (p = 0.00293). In insurance-claim approval, Opus 5 re-checks on its own and succeeds on 10/10, whereas Claude Sonnet 5 rises from 1/10 to 9/10 when the current policy is shown again, a gain significant in its pre-registered test but not after correcting for all tests. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Workshops (AABA4ET and CL4FMAgents)</p></details> |
| **[Engineering process plants as code in the age of agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40239v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) could one day shorten process plant engineering from years to weeks. On this foundation, Design-Ops extends DevOps principles to plant engineering, linking every engineering item to the information it derives from and replacing manual checking with automated tests. Agents can then coordinate routine work across disciplines while deterministic tools perform the calculations. Verification sets the pace: agents propose, tools check, and engineers review and authorize every change. We outline the risks of this shift and the research, industry and policy actions it requires.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 2 figures. Submitted to Nature Chemical Engineering as a Comment</p></details> |
| **[When Is Smaller Greener? Right-Sizing Language Models for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19345v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents use a large language model (LLM) to plan and act over many steps. Which model should an agent use, so that its tasks are completed reliably with the least energy, cost and time? We treat this as a multi-criteria decision problem in which comparisons per call can mislead: a task succeeds only if all its steps succeed, and failed attempts still use resources. We therefore compare models on resources per successfully completed task. We derive crossover task lengths at which two models exchange their efficiency ranking, determined by their per-step reliability and resource use. Each objective has its own crossover. Below the shortest crossover the less reliable model dominates, above the longest the more reliable one dominates, and in between both are Pareto-efficient and preferences decide. This supports a two-stage framework: a preference-free dominance screen, followed by a multi-criteria ranking of the efficient models, subject to reliability and feasibility constraints. We also give conditions under which this structure holds when reliability and resource use change during a task. In a pilot with 15 open-weight models on two synthetic agent tasks, with measured GPU energy, the predicted crossover fell within the observed range for 72 of the 88 observed crossovers. At 20 steps, a 3-billion-parameter model used a tenth of the energy of a 27-billion one per completed task; at 100 steps, a 1.7-billion model used almost four times as much as an 8-billion one. Smaller is greener only up to a task length that can be predicted.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoGround: Self-Evolving Video Agents for Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13803v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video temporal grounding (VTG) takes an untrimmed video and a natural-language query as input and localizes the temporal moment that best matches the query. Trained on 2.5K unlabeled videos, EvoGround matches or surpasses most fully supervised models across five VTG benchmarks, while emerging as a state-of-the-art fine-grained video captioner. | **[EpiAgent: Epistemic Self-Play for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40227v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Epistemic game theory explains an agent's strategic behavior via the beliefs it holds about others and how these beliefs are updated as the interactions unfold. Modern LLM-based agents, however, often lack an explicit mechanism for learning and updating such beliefs to guide decisions under incomplete information. To bridge this gap, we propose EpiAgent, a framework that equips LLM-based agents with an explicit, learnable epistemic layer, which maintains beliefs over others' private information and combines these beliefs with the agent's available information into an epistemic state for action selection. Concretely, the agent's policy is decomposed into two coupled stages: Epistemic Update, which updates these beliefs from interaction histories, and Action Planning, which selects actions conditioned on the updated epistemic state. By formulating their dependency as a bi-level optimization problem, we introduce Epistemic Self-Play, an algorithm that alternately optimizes the two stages through self-play, utilizing an intrinsic prediction reward to evaluate how well inferred beliefs predict subsequent observations. Empirical results across both mixed and competitive scenarios show that EpiAgent consistently outperforms prompting-based and RL-based baselines. | **[Video Generation Meets Agent Harness: Agentic Video Generation, Agentic World Modeling and Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40191v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video generation is shifting from end-to-end synthesis toward agentic generation, where planning, tool use, and feedback enable coherent, controllable multi-shot videos. This report examines three directions. First, agentic video generation, where harnesses coordinate scripting, shot planning, asset management, production, and editing, alongside the evaluation challenges of these systems. Second, agentic world modeling, where harnesses mediate observation, control, execution, and state. We discuss hybrid designs in which game engines or agent-written programs maintain world state while video models render observations, and how agents and world models can mutually evaluate and improve one another. Third, recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video model development, distinguishing output revisions, updates to harness state, generator parameters, and evaluators, and changes to the improvement procedure itself. We identify how these improvements feed back into subsequent development. Across these directions, we analyze essential agent capabilities, including audiovisual perception, long-horizon planning, memory, code-based interaction, feedback-guided control, skill reuse, and multi-agent coordination. We conclude with challenges in autonomous video production, lifelong memory, programmable hybrid control, autonomous model development, provenance, cost accounting, and evaluation linking video quality and task performance to sustained improvements in models and harnesses.</p></details> | <details><summary>websi...</summary><p>website: https://agentic-videogen.github.io/ github: https://github.com/agentic-videogen/awesome-agentic-videogen</p></details> |
| **[Locating and Closing Information Gaps in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40186v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-step Web tasks often require several LLM agents to gather and combine evidence. The communication graph of a fixed workflow restricts which agents can read each other's outputs: evidence retrieved by one agent may never reach the agent that needs it, leaving an information gap and an incomplete or incorrect answer. Adding a connection can deliver the missing evidence, but can also lead the receiver to adopt an incorrect upstream conclusion that displaces a correct answer; the final output alone does not separate the two. We propose Selective Probing and Link Insertion for Closing information gaps in multi-agent Execution (SPLICE), which locates information gaps per query and decides what to adopt after adding an edge. After the original workflow runs, SPLICE uses the query and node outputs to locate a possible gap, adds one directed edge from a source to a receiver to deliver the relevant evidence, and re-executes the receiver and affected downstream nodes, producing a candidate answer. The adoption decision reads both the final answers of the two runs and the process changes the edit caused inside the workflow: how far the receiver moved, where its new content came from, and whether the change reached the final answer. A selector trained on these process features chooses between adopting the candidate answer and retaining the original. On five benchmarks, SPLICE raises the task score on Web tool use by 8.0% and the mean score on four reasoning and planning benchmarks by 5.7% relative to the static skeleton, at 1.25 to 1.44$\times$ the skeleton's cost, and transfers zero-shot to four unseen backbones.</p></details> |  |

| **[Heavy-Tailed Memory Traces in Long-Horizon Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00010v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon language agents are becoming increasingly important for tasks that require persistent interaction, planning, and reasoning over extended trajectories. | **[World Model Science: Self-Organized Criticality, Weak Chaos, and Metastable Belief Dynamics in Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17419v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon LLM agents must maintain and update a world model over extended sequences of observations, actions, decisions and tool calls. | **[When Do Causal World Models Help Modular LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00012v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly operate in modular systems, where planning requires coordinating multiple functional components whose actions can causally change downstream states and behaviors. To address this limitation, we propose CausalCompose, a causal world-model framework that equips modular LLM agents with causal structure for intervention-aware prediction and planning. Our results show when causal world models help modular LLM agents: when causal dependencies are identifiable and actionable for planning.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[Agentic process evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40071v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The way software gets built is changing dramatically. AI agents now create pull requests, select dependencies, refactor codebases, interpret test failures, and execute infrastructure changes across the entire Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC). AI agents are autonomous team members, but unlike human engineers, they are ephemeral - their reasoning, decisions, and tool calls vanish the moment the session ends. Modern Security incidents involving intent mismatch or injection are becoming increasingly common - forcing us to address AI Agents as an insider threat. No widely adopted standard yet enables agentic provenance and governance and its obvious that you can't hold an ephemeral process accountable. The git log was designed for human commits. It shows what changed. It does not show what an AI agent did, which model it used, which tools it called, or whether anyone was accountable for the outcome. Every governance framework, every audit trail, every compliance control in the SDLC was built around one assumption: a human made the decision. That assumption no longer holds. This shift introduces 3 main procedural problems (not addressing scale) - Supply-chain vector - The agentic coder is software, so it is a supply-chain security risk. Policy guardrails - How to ensure the agentic coder is compliant with the policies of the organization. Task-alignment - How to align the agentic coder on its tasks and goals. We introduce Agentic Process Evidence (APE), a signed, tamper-evident, machine-readable attestation of agentic processes, linking the sessions to their outcome (process-aware logging). APE enables Policy-as-Code control over the agentic processes, allowing for human in the loop only when it's needed. We support the in-toto style attestation framework, and the protocol is aligned with the relevant regulations</p></details> |  |
| **[CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents. The project repository is available at https://github.com/OpenCausaLab/CauProphet.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages, 9 figures, including appendices</p></details> |

Experiments on seven general-domain and multi-hop question answering benchmarks demonstrate that \textsc{ZEST-Search} consistently improves search-agent performance while achieving higher tool efficiency without labeled search-training data or expert search trajectories. | **[Learning to Formulate: Agentic Hardware-Software Co-Design of AI Infrastructure through Progressive Optimization and Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39380v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI infrastructure, the chips and software that train and serve AI models, is designed in layers. Wall relaxation searches the chip by pricing the hardware limits that bind the software. This paper takes a first step, through separate studies rather than one end-to-end run. ## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[A Co-Design Approach to Physically-Informed Molecular Docking for Quantum Annealers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09540v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecular docking is a crucial step in computer- aided drug design, involving the identification of ligand poses that are physically plausible within a protein pocket. Recent studies have shown that this problem can be formulated as a geometric weighted subgraph isomorphism, which can then be encoded as a QUBO and thus solved on D-Wave quantum annealers. In this work, we adopt a co-design approach, addressing molecular docking at the formulation and encoding levels, and examining how hardware topology affects solution quality on current quantum annealers. We propose new QUBO formulations that extend the state-of-the-art ones by including van der Waals, Coulomb, hydrogen bonding, and hydrophobic interactions. Experimental results show that incorporating physicochemical terms improves the average docking quality by more than 18% compared to the purely geometric baseline on D-Wave quantum annealers with the Pegasus topology, and by up to 29% on those with the Zephyr topology. The choice of topology, along with an improved encoding design, yields significant gains in embedding efficiency and the number of valid solutions. Our designs reduce the number of physical qubits required by up to 14.5% and decrease the chain break fraction by an order of magnitude, thereby enhancing confidence in the resulting solutions.</p></details> | 7 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Latent Style-based Quantum Wasserstein GAN for Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22399v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The development of new drugs is a time-consuming and expensive process, with average costs estimated to be up to around $2.5 billion. The first step in this process is the design of the new drug, for which de novo drug design, assisted by artificial intelligence, has blossomed in recent years and has revolutionized the field. In particular, generative artificial intelligence has delivered promising results in drug discovery and development, reducing costs and time to solution. However, classical generative models, such as generative adversarial networks (GANs), can be difficult to train and are prone to mode collapse. Quantum computing may be an avenue to overcome some of these issues, providing compact models with far fewer trainable parameters, thereby enhancing the generalizability of GANs. We propose a new latent style-based quantum Wasserstein GAN (QGAN) for drug design, combining a variational autoencoder that maps molecular structures into a latent space with a hybrid quantum GAN. Our pipeline is benchmarked against classical models of the industry-relevant MOSES suite. Style-based noise reuploading at every rotational gate and a Wasserstein gradient penalty are used to improve trainability and to mitigate mode collapse. Training and large-scale sampling with circuits of up to 15 qubits are performed on noiseless quantum simulators to validate our architecture. We present an inference-only demonstration on an IBM Heron quantum computer using 5 qubits, sampling 2,500 new molecules, to investigate quantum noise effects. Our default QGAN, using 110 trainable generator parameters, reaches statistically comparable overall performance and statistically significant gains on selected drug-relevant metrics relative to both the MOSES classical latent GAN baseline, which contains about 700,000 trainable parameters for the generator, and a classical latent GAN with matched generator capacity.</p></details> | <details><summary>Main ...</summary><p>Main part: 26 pages, 11 figures, 6 tables. Supplementary material: 24 pages, 20 figures, 21 tables. v2: revision containing matched-capacity analysis of the classical GAN and extensive details on the hardware inference run, plus bootstrap error analysis of the hardware results. Matches published version</p></details> |

We introduce Matcha, which refines a ligand pose by three flow-matching stages in translation, rotation and torsion space, then minimizes, filters and ranks candidates with GNINA. Across the benchmarks considered, Matcha attains the strongest physical plausibility and runs about $28\times$ faster than modern large-scale co-folding models. Code and benchmarking artifacts are available at https://github.com/LigandPro/Matcha and archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23568093. Model checkpoints are archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23569055.</p></details> |  |
| **[MLIPDiff: Guiding Denoising Diffusion Models for Structure-Based Drug Design via Machine-Learned Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33315v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion models have become a dominant approach to structure-based drug design. However, because they are trained primarily to fit reference structures, they often place ligands in physically implausible poses that clash with the protein. We introduce MLIPDiff, an inference-time guidance method that can be applied to pretrained diffusion models for ligand generation. The method uses physics-informed guidance from UMA (Universal Models for Atoms), a machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP). The guidance combines four weighted energy terms (protein-ligand interaction, ligand strain, steric clash, and pocket anchoring) and uses the original model checkpoint without retraining. On CrossDocked2020, MLIPDiff completely eliminates protein-ligand collisions while preserving the ligand's local geometry, improving bond-length distributions and physical plausibility relative to the base model. Compared to models such as NucleusDiff that are specifically designed to reduce atomic clashes, MLIPDiff achieves clash-free structures without compromising local geometry. It maintains the base model's PoseBusters pass rate and attains the best median Vina Score, Vina Min, and Vina Dock, surpassing both the base model and NucleusDiff, with a median of 59.6\% of ligands per pocket binding more strongly than the reference. MLIPDiff thus addresses a limitation of prior methods by generating physically plausible structures with favorable predicted binding affinity using a single frozen model and no additional training.</p></details> |  |
| **[Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. | **[What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Our results also show that our approach is data-efficient and insensitive to example balance, including the ability to learn from exclusively positive examples.</p></details> |  |

## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[NeuroPareto: Calibrated Acquisition for Costly Many-Goal Search in Vast Parameter Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03901v7)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The pursuit of optimal trade-offs in high-dimensional search spaces under stringent computational constraints poses a fundamental challenge for contemporary multi-objective optimization. We develop NeuroPareto, a cohesive architecture that integrates rank-centric filtering, uncertainty disentanglement, and history-conditioned acquisition strategies to navigate complex objective landscapes. A calibrated Bayesian classifier estimates epistemic uncertainty across non-domination tiers, enabling rapid generation of high-quality candidates with minimal evaluation cost. Experiments on DTLZ and ZDT suites and a subsurface energy extraction task show that NeuroPareto consistently outperforms classifier-enhanced and surrogate-assisted baselines in Pareto proximity and hypervolume.</p></details> | 39 pages, 19 figures |

Experimental results demonstrate that EGD outperforms task-specific conditional generators in property targeting; SAES preserves structural diversity while retaining competitive hypervolume in multi-objective optimization; and DEMO improves objective convergence and generates diverse feasible molecules under fragment constraints. These results show how evolutionary search can reuse existing diffusion models for diverse molecular design requirements.</p></details> |  |

On this problem class, we analyze the runtime of GSEMO and the widely used NSGA-II. | **[Instant Software Factories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36254v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents can quickly build prototypes, but a long tail of fixes, optimizations, tests, and releases separates a prototype from a product. Our framework introduces three key contributions: (1) a three-stage curriculum for stable multi-objective optimization, (2) an enhanced Batch-Contrastive loss for more discriminative feature learning, and (3) two novel regularizers, Answer-Contrastive (AC) loss to refine the prediction space and Gradient-Discrepancy (GD) loss to enforce causal visual grounding. Our model achieves a competitive accuracy of 61.64% on the bias-sensitive VQA-CP v2 benchmark while maintaining 62.80% on the standard VQA v2 dataset, yielding a small generalization gap of 1.16%. This demonstrates a strong balance between OOD robustness and in-distribution performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication at the 30th International Conference on Knowledge-Based and Intelligent Information & Engineering Systems (KES 2026). 9 pages, 5 figures</p></details> |
| **[DQN-Scheduler: A Multi-Objective Optimization Framework for Scheduling Microservices in Cloud Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14117v1)** | 2026-09-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud computing has emerged as an information technology solution, providing software and infrastructure solutions for companies and individuals. The pay-as-you-go approach has increased demands for the cloud. The massive range of resources, the variety of services, and flexible pricing grab attention. In addition, microservices have emerged as a new way of building software, with applications developed as loosely dependent tasks. Additionally, container technology has boosted the popularity of microservices by offering a platform for this type of architecture. Containers and microservices improve the flexibility and scalability of cloud applications. There are two primary types of microservices: batch and online services, with the majority of applications falling into the online service category. Scheduling microservices is challenging because it requires careful management of resource utilization, load balancing, network latency, reliability, and availability. In this study, we introduce the DQN-Scheduler, a novel reinforcement learning-based agent designed to optimize microservice scheduling in cloud environments. Our approach aims to optimize multiple scheduling objectives simultaneously, such as resource utilization, load balancing, latency, reliability, and availability. To our knowledge, this is the first framework to address all these objectives simultaneously. The DQN-Scheduler was tested against benchmark algorithms in the field. The experimental results demonstrate that the DQN-Scheduler outperforms benchmark algorithms.</p></details> |  |

To enable low-complexity online decision-making, a heterogeneous graph neural network (GNN) is developed to learn the joint mapping from estimated polarized CSI to digital beamforming and transmit/receive polarforming vectors (PFVs) while accounting for the system constraints. | **[GeoTTER: Leveraging Local Geometry of Optimal Transport for Zero-Shot Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13518v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present GeoTTER, a novel framework that redefines optimal transport in the realm of zero-shot classification. Conventional methods often suffer from miscalibration and a lack of adaptability, as they rely on fixed cost matrices derived solely from pre-trained model embeddings. First, to alleviate high-frequency label jaggedness (sample-level manifold jitter that assigns neighboring embeddings to different classes), GeoTTER integrates local geometric structure into the optimal transport formulation via graph-Laplacian smoothing, a technique grounded in spectral graph theory that enforces neighborhood consistency. | **[SIMS: Scale-Invariant Merit-Function-Based Scalarization for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12599v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-task learning (MTL) requires navigating unavoidable trade-offs among competing objectives. We empirically find that existing merit-function-based scalarization approaches are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives in practical MTL, where task losses commonly differ by orders of magnitude. The optimization process often favors objectives with larger scales even though the underlying Pareto optimal solutions remains invariant to rescaling (i.e., multiplying an objective by a positive constant). To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. We further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error. Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the 32nd ACM SIGKDD Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (KDD 2026)</p></details> |
| **[ParetoTransport: Generative Optimization by Mass Transport Toward The Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07706v1)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline multi-objective optimization requires not only moving the objective vectors of candidate designs toward the Pareto front, but also distributing them effectively along it. | **[Data-Driven Design Optimization of Streaming-Potential-Mediated Electrokinetic Transport of Viscoelastic Fluids in Microchannels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29939v1)** | 2026-08-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Streaming-potential-mediated transport of viscoelastic fluids has attracted research attention owing to its applications in electrokinetic energy conversion and microfluidic transport. The proposed methodology can significantly accelerate parametric exploration compared with repeated numerical simulations across different parameters and provides practical design guidelines for electrokinetic microfluidic devices. The study demonstrates the potential of combining computational fluid mechanics with data-driven surrogate modeling for efficient engineering design and optimization.</p></details> |  |

Within this unified framework, we instantiate KENDO-BO for BO and KENDO-AL for Bayesian AL, demonstrating that both arise from a common self-correcting mechanism with task-specific acquisition objectives. We further extend the approach to multi-objective optimization via random scalarization that preserves the single-optimizer conditioning structure.
