| **[ATLAS: Automated Approximation of Transformers for Efficient Homomorphic Inference in One Hour](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23478v2)** | 2026-08-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) lets a server run inference on encrypted data with strong privacy guarantees, but running a Transformer under FHE is expensive. Its non-linear operations, such as softmax, normalization, and activation, must be replaced with polynomial approximations that the CKKS scheme supports, and the depth of these approximations dominates inference cost. Existing FHE Transformers use hand-tuned approximation settings, such as iteration count and polynomial degree, applied uniformly across layers, models, and tasks. Hand-tuning is slow and error-prone. Even a single uniform setting has about $10^7$ choices, and manual search cannot exploit layer-wise variation. AutoFHE, the only automated method with multi-objective search, targets ReLU-only CNNs and needs full fine-tuning per candidate, which is too costly for Transformers. Per-layer settings also push the search space to about $10^{85}$ for BERT and ViT and $10^{228}$ for LLaMA3, beyond both manual and fine-tuning-based search. We present ATLAS, a training-free framework that automates this search by treating each layer's approximation setting as a multi-objective optimization over latency and accuracy. The problem is hard: the decision space is large (96 or 256 variables), each configuration takes 70 to 1,000 seconds to evaluate even in cleartext, and 85 to 90 percent of configurations are invalid. ATLAS handles this with a two-stage optimization strategy and a surrogate model, completing the search in about one hour. Compared to an iterative softmax baseline, ATLAS cuts multiplicative depth and end-to-end latency by about 35 percent with little accuracy loss, and works across encoder-only, decoder-only, and vision Transformers, complementing parallel work on packing and matrix multiplication.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/jianhayes/ATLAS</p></details> |
| **[Primal--Dual Alternating Neural Learning for Timely Classification with Performance Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23480v1)** | 2026-08-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Timely risk classification is essential in many clinical monitoring settings, where decisions must balance the benefit of classifying patients early for subsequent intervention against the value of observing additional data. Yet most existing statistical and machine-learning methods are designed for fully observed trajectories and offer limited control over key operating characteristics such as sensitivity, specificity, and monitoring cost. We cast the sequential classification problem within a multi-objective optimization framework targeting these three criteria. We characterize the optimal decision rule through a value recursion that quantifies, at each time point, the trade-off between immediate classification and continued monitoring. To estimate the rule from data, we formulate a constrained optimization problem that maximizes specificity while enforcing prespecified sensitivity and monitoring-cost constraints. We then develop an estimation procedure that employs a recurrent neural network to approximate the evolving value processes and a primal--dual updating scheme to satisfy the performance constraints. Through simulation studies and an application to continuous glucose monitoring for hypoglycemia risk prediction, we demonstrate that the proposed method yields accurate and timely sequential decision rules that adhere to the desired operating characteristics.</p></details> |  |

## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[MacKay-Neal Codes for DNA Channels under Block-MAP Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38440v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We prove achievability below the symmetric information rate (SIR) under block maximum a posteriori (MAP) decoding for a fixed-degree MacKay-Neal (MN) socket ensemble on the quaternary insertion-deletion-substitution channel used to model DNA reads. For every integer $\ell\ge4$, the $(\ell,3,3)$ socket ensemble, with one transmitted bit fixed and an independent uniform known offset, has limiting rate $r_\star=6/\ell$ bits per stored nucleotide. If the joint SIR $J_t$ of a fixed number $t$ of reads under independent, uniformly distributed input nucleotides exceeds $r_\star$, the ensemble-average full extended-word block error is $O(1/n)$, and deterministic graph-offset sequences attain the same limiting rate and message-average error bound. This result concerns the discrete rate family $6/\ell$, not arbitrary rates approaching a given SIR. The channel permits arbitrarily many insertions and unbounded drift, with no alignment supplied to the receiver. The proof combines established MN parity and distance estimates with a strict arbitrary-candidate-list bound and a list construction that accounts for hidden output boundaries.</p></details> | 14 pages |
| **[Peak-DNA: Recurrent Morphology in Global Narrative Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Global narrative streams exhibit noisy, fragmented, and asynchronous amplification bursts, making direct waveform comparison unreliable for recurrence analysis. This paper introduces Peak-DNA, a framework for identifying recurrent propagation morphology in global narrative streams. The proposed framework transforms hourly GDELT activity into normalized macro-peak episodes through denoising, macro-peak consolidation, and temporal alignment, and compares propagation morphology using DTW-based similarity analysis. Using GDELT narrative streams, we analyze five high-amplification narrative domains: Iran, Defense, AI, Space/Elon, and Quantum/PQC. The results show that amplification events repeatedly converge toward morphology-level propagation patterns within each narrative domain. DTW-based similarity analysis further demonstrates strong intra-domain structural compactness despite heterogeneous publication dynamics and severe observational noise. These findings suggest a measurable morphology layer beyond semantic content or anomaly intensity, providing an initial structural foundation for recurrence-aware narrative monitoring, propagation family discovery, and longitudinal Narrative Genome analysis.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages. Accepted and presented at ASONAM 2026, Rabat, Morocco, August 24-27, 2026. Proceedings publication forthcoming</p></details> |
| **[CCP-DP: Cross-cluster context-guided phase dynamic programming for DNA storage sequence reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36559v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivation: DNA data storage offers high information density and long-term stability, but accurate sequence reconstruction remains challenging under limited sequencing coverage and strong insertion, deletion, and substitution (IDS) noise. Existing reconstruction methods mainly rely on local evidence within individual read clusters. When sequencing coverage is low or indel-induced phase shifts become pronounced, such local evidence may be insufficient to distinguish closely competing candidate sequences. Results: We present Cross-Cluster Context-Guided Phase Dynamic Programming (CCP-DP), which uses dataset-level cross-cluster information to complement cluster-local evidence and selectively refines ambiguous provisional consensuses. Under the main benchmark settings, CCP-DP achieved exact reconstruction rates of 95.36%-99.98% and outperformed existing methods on multiple datasets. At $5\times$ coverage, CCP-DP improved exact reconstruction by 6.2-17.5 percentage points over the stronger of BBS and CPL across the tested IDS conditions, while maintaining low reconstruction error and computational overhead. Overall, CCP-DP introduces cross-cluster shared information as a complementary source of evidence for DNA sequence reconstruction under challenging conditions. Availability: Source code is available at https://github.com/ZixinZ3899/CCP-DP.</p></details> |  |
| **[ACOR: incremental evidence accumulation towards real-time DNA storage reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36500v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA offers high-density, durable storage by encoding digital information into synthetic strands and retrieving it through sequencing. Sequence reconstruction combines noisy copies of each strand before downstream decoding. Conventional offline methods typically reconstruct completed read clusters, separating reconstruction from ongoing read collection. Streaming reconstruction offers a way to overlap evidence processing with sequencing and reduce the computation remaining before downstream decoding. We present ACOR, a streaming consensus reconstruction method that records $k$-mer transitions and boundary support in a persistent ledger, then decodes through length-constrained beam search and priority-based selection at cluster closure. Evaluation on real and simulated DNA-storage datasets showed competitive recovery. On our own short-read next-generation sequencing (NGS) wet-lab library, ACOR combined near-complete recovery with second-scale reconstruction of more than ten million reads. A smaller public benchmark was completed in a fraction of a second. Parallel ACOR achieved speedups of tens to over a thousand-fold relative to the tested serial baseline configurations. Sparse storage reduced high-context memory requirements, while parallel execution accelerated reconstruction computation. ACOR is designed for streaming readout pipelines that process assigned reads while sequencing continues.</p></details> |  |
| **[Enhancing Clinical Classifier Robustness with Deep Representation Learning Based OOD Detection on DNA Methylation Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36344v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA methylation (DNAM) array-based tumor classification is a powerful clinical diagnostic tool, but models trained on fixed reference cohorts can produce faulty predictions for tumor types not represented in the training distribution. Deep neural network (DNN) classifiers were trained on public sarcoma (SARC) (GSE140686) and brain tumor (BT) (GSE90496) cohorts, and feature vectors were extracted from bottleneck layers. Five OOD methods, including 1dimension (1D)-Subspace Analysis (1D-SSA), K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), Ridge Regression, Mahalanobis Distance (MD), and Sum of Squared Distances (SSD), were explored with 200 and 500-dimensional embeddings. Spectral discrepancy was used to discriminate in-distribution (IND) from OOD samples. Performance was measured via cross-validation, holdout samples, and an independent clinical dataset. 1D-SSA was the most effective approach. In the sarcoma-IND/brain tumor-OOD setting, the framework achieved an AUROC of 0.975 and True Negative Rate (TNR)@95TPR of 0.930, and an AUROC of 0.9569 and TNR@95TPR of 0.8660 for the brain tumor-IND/sarcoma-OOD setting. These results show deep representation-based OOD detection as a practical quality-control layer for clinical-grade methylation-based tumor classification systems.</p></details> | 11 pages, 8 figures |
| **[Data-Driven Soft Labeling Scales DNA Read Classification to Whole-Body Cell-Type Deconvolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04987v5)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cell-type deconvolution, the task of estimating the proportions of constituent cell types in a heterogeneous biological sample, is a core problem in computational biology. Methods that rely on epigenetic marks such as DNA methylation typically operate on aggregated methylation estimates, discarding the pattern-level information carried by individual DNA reads. Existing read-level approaches that exploit this information are scarce, and all remain restricted to few-class settings; scaling them further is an open problem because, at scale, non-discriminative reads dominate and hard labels conflict with the many-to-many mapping between methylation patterns and cell types, preventing classifier convergence. To overcome this, we propose data-driven soft labels that estimate the conditional cell-type distribution for each read, and integrate this scheme into $Syto$, a new modular framework for read-level classification-based deconvolution. On a whole-body atlas of 39 human cell types, $Syto$ reduces MSE by 3.7$\times$ over the best examined baseline, with gains transferring to an out-of-distribution dataset spanning 16 tissues. $Syto$ lays the foundation for modeling increasingly large cell-type panels, with improved applications in biology and healthcare. The proposed soft-labeling scheme is further translatable to any setting with a many-to-many signal-to-label mapping.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Track), poster</p></details> |
| **[Secure Multimodal Federated Optimization via Hierarchical DNA-Based Key Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27644v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated optimization over heterogeneous multimodal data enables distributed institutions to collaboratively optimize a shared model using medical images, genomic profiles, clinical records, and physiological signals without centralizing raw data. However, protecting modality-specific data throughout the optimization lifecycle remains insufficiently explored, particularly when heterogeneous modalities require separate encryption keys and selective local access. This paper proposes a secure multimodal federated optimization framework that separates local data protection from federated-update protection. A trusted key-management authority securely provisions one client key through Hybrid Public Key Encryption (HPKE), from which each client locally derives modality-specific symmetric keys using the domain-separated HMAC-based Extract-and-Expand Key Derivation Function (HKDF). Heterogeneous modalities are mapped into a unified DNA-domain representation and processed through a DNA-origami-inspired structural transformation before authenticated encryption with AES-GCM. During local optimization, only the required modalities are decrypted within an authorized client environment. Individual model updates are independently protected through secure aggregation, allowing the coordinator to observe only aggregated updates rather than each client's contribution. Together, these components establish a structured approach to modality-specific protection and secure collaborative optimization, integrating hierarchical key management, authenticated local encryption, and aggregation-level update protection within a unified framework.</p></details> |  |
| **[Representing Information on DNA using Patterns Induced by Enzymatic Labeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24171v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enzymatic DNA labeling is a powerful tool with applications in biochemistry, molecular biology, biotechnology, medical science, and genomic research. This paper contributes to the evolving field of DNA-based data storage by presenting a formal framework for modeling DNA labeling in strings, specifically tailored for data storage purposes. Our approach involves a known DNA molecule as a template for labeling, employing patterns induced by a set of designed labels to represent information. One hypothetical implementation can use CRISPR-Cas9 and gRNA reagents for labeling. Various aspects of the general labeling channel, including fixed-length labels, are explored, and upper bounds on the maximum size of the corresponding codes are given. The study includes the development of an efficient encoder-decoder pair that is proven to be optimal in terms of maximum code size under specific conditions. Finally, for the non-random access setting, we derive new lower bounds and constructions that characterize the asymptotic behavior of the expected number of samples required to recover all information strands.</p></details> |  |

We compare ART with LoRA and soft prompting across five reasoning benchmarks for four small MLLMs from three architectures. | **[TextSeal2: Distortion-Free LLM Watermarking with Copula Mixing and Memoryless Races](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40613v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present TextSeal2, a distortion-free watermark for large language models that keeps its signal under speculative decoding: tokens that replace rejected drafts remain watermarked with the same keys, and the acceptance rate is unchanged. Experiments across nine LLMs show that models that initially respond appropriately can shift toward ethical violations over extended interactions, with substantial variation across models and ethical dimensions. These results show that single-turn evaluations may overestimate the ethical reliability of LLM-based mental health support systems and highlight the need to evaluate ethical behavior throughout multi-turn interaction.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking Bayesian Optimization for Co-Optimizing LLM Training Configurations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08351v3)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-tuning an LLM to maximize performance on a downstream task requires finding the optimal data and model configuration. This is a black-box optimization problem that Bayesian optimization (BO) addresses by sequentially evaluating training configurations and using observed performance feedback to adaptively guide the search towards better configurations. However, directly applying BO to the joint data-model space suffers from two fundamental challenges: the prohibitive cost of full LLM training at each iteration, and the large number of BO iterations required for effective exploration due to the high problem dimensionality. This paper introduces JoBS, a BO-based approach that co-optimizes data and model configurations without requiring a full training run at every iteration. JoBS allocates an initial portion of the optimization budget to learn a scaling-law-inspired performance predictor that estimates fully trained LLM performance from only a small number of training steps. The remaining budget is then used to run BO with this predictor, eliminating the need for full training runs and enabling JoBS to explore significantly more data and model configurations within the same budget. We analyze JoBS's average regret and derive the optimal budget allocation between predictor learning and BO iterations. Empirically, JoBS outperforms independent data and model optimization methods and existing multi-fidelity BO baselines across a diverse set of downstream tasks. | **[Holistic Diversity Management in LLM-Guided Evolutionary Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38407v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-guided evolutionary search iteratively selects existing programs, uses LLMs as mutation operators to generate a new program, and evaluates it to guide subsequent search. To preserve diversity and reduce the risk of premature convergence, it often adopts the island model, in which groups of programs, called islands, evolve separately. However, this structure alone does not guarantee distinct search directions, as islands may start from similar programs or converge toward similar solutions during evolution. This suggests that diversity must be considered throughout the search process, from initializing islands with distinct programs to developing island trajectories and sharing discoveries across islands. To this end, we propose HoliDivEvolve , a holistic approach to establishing and effectively utilizing island diversity throughout evolutionary search. Specifically, we introduce diverse initialization to establish different initial programs, trajectory adaptive island selection to allocate computation based on current score, recent growth, and the remaining search budget, and growth adaptive migration to share alternative ideas across islands. Experiments across diverse benchmarks show that HoliDivEvolve achieves competitive performance on most evaluated tasks. We further conduct controlled analyses to examine how our design choices affect search behavior and final performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLMs as Judges and Examinees for Medical Item Difficulty Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38402v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting medical item difficulty from item text alone remains challenging. As simulated examinees, they generated response-based signals including correctness, keyed-option probability, response entropy, and the probability margin between the top two options. We then combined the strongest judgment and response signals with baseline item features in downstream prediction models. Pairwise difficulty judgment was the most consistent individual LLM signal across both datasets, while simulated-examinee signals and absolute judgments provided complementary information in some feature combinations. The best configurations reached a Spearman correlation of $ρ$ = .447 on USMLE, exceeding the best system in the 2024 BEA shared task ($ρ$ = .25), and $ρ$ = .494 on PANCE. These results were comparable to direct absolute judgments from the proprietary frontier model Claude Opus 5 ($ρ$ = .443 and .488, respectively). Overall, judgment and response signals from open-weight LLMs captured difficulty-related information beyond text representations. Because these models can be deployed locally, the approach offers a practical option for predicting the difficulty of unreleased or high-security test items.</p></details> |  |
| **[Uncovering Why LLMs Fail: From Internal Mechanisms to Adversarial Reasoning Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37369v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are usually evaluated by final-output correctness or safety, yet an endpoint label does not reveal how a failure develops throughout the reasoning process. Towards this end, we in this paper study a white-box diagnostic framework CauTrace to disclose the root-cause of why LLM fails from three fundamental inspections: Functional States, Propagation of Representation, Temporal-State Coordination and Reasoning Trajectory. First, seven functional states are creatively defined to represent the support and constraint processing in LLMs. Second, the propagation of representation change measures how the changes among the seven states persist across subsequent Transformer blocks. Third, temporal-state coordination and trajectory topology characterize when the commitment and verification occur relative to the support, and how the dominant state sequence evolves over the full inference process. Finally, we evaluate CauTrace on both clean reasoning failures and paired adversarial perturbations. Across Qwen and LLaMA-family models, the failed traces show weaker Attention-MLP agreement on support-state updates and less verification activity relative to commitment, while the exact representation-level signatures and timing shifts vary by architecture. Held-out repair and steering tests demonstrate consistent operational signals that were in line with our claims for certain models and specific settings, with aligned steering producing the predicted directional changes in process measures. Overall, CauTrace provides a unified diagnostic framework for analyzing failure dynamics through changes in support, commitment, and verification, which would shed light on the in-depth research on LLM security.</p></details> |  |
| **[Compute Has Shape: Self-Morphing the Internal Computation of Frozen LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38382v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Test-time scaling improves large language models through additional computation, but how to adapt the topology of internal computation to individual inputs remains underexplored. Existing approaches largely fix recurrent or parallel architectures during training, or reconfigure frozen models along a single structural axis. It unifies the original forward path (BASE), serial reuse of a layer span (REPEAT), and local branching and merging of perturbed hidden states (BRANCH) in a unified space parameterized by type, location, and intensity. Since they offer complementary benefits rather than uniform dominance, we use a lightweight selector that reads the backbone's depth-wise hidden state trajectory on the prompt to choose a morphology before response generation. We train the selector by distilling utility-based distribution from offline morphing that jointly account for task performance and computational cost. Evaluated on three LLMs spanning 4B-27B, SelfMorph achieves a 8.06% mean relative improvement on five in-distribution benchmarks and 4.45% on four unseen benchmarks. It achieves the strongest performance in 24 of 27 settings. These findings identify input-adaptive computational topology as a complementary axis of test-time scaling beyond generating more tokens. Our code is available at https://github.com/creator-xi/SelfMorph.</p></details> |  |
| **[Few-Shot Fairness: Unveiling LLM's Potential for Fairness-Aware Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.18502v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being employed for downstream tasks such as tabular classification, where predictions can directly influence individuals in high-stakes domain such as hiring, education and criminal justice. As the adoption of LLMs for such decision making tasks continues to grow, ensuring fairness in their predictions becomes increasingly important. Existing approaches to ensure fairness in tabular data, typically process test instances independently, limiting the models awareness of the demographic composition and predictions of other instances, which is particularly important for reasoning about group-level fairness. To address this limitation, we propose FABLE (FAir Batch-based LLM framEwork) for tabular classification, a batch-based framework that jointly processes multiple test instances, enabling LLMs to reason over demographic information and prediction outcomes within a batch. To mitigate positional bias introduced by batch processing, the framework generates multiple shuffled versions of each batch and aggregates the resulting predictions through voting. We evaluate FABLE on three tabular classification datasets using three popular LLMs and four prompting configurations spanning zero-shot, few-shot, direct, and self-reflective prompting. Experimental results demonstrate that incorporating demographic information and explicit fairness constraints enable LLMs to achieve substantial fairness improvements across diverse datasets and fairness notions, highlighting their potential as fairness reasoners for tabular classification and promoting more equitable and accountable decision-making.</p></details> |  |
| **[FlatCake: A Flat KVCache Storage Architecture for LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38355v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>KV cache reuse accelerates LLM serving, but existing systems manage host DRAM and NVMe SSD as a tiered hierarchy where DRAM acts as an intermediate cache. Production LLM workloads break both premises of this hierarchy. First, bimodal reuse defeats DRAM temporal filtering, since workloads are dominated either by one-shot blocks with long reuse distances or by multi-gigabyte agentic sessions that exceed DRAM capacity, putting SSD on the critical read path. Second, DRAM staging and eviction-ordered layout bottleneck SSD restoration. Holding host staging buffers for in-flight prefixes causes contention under load that aborts prefetches, while incremental eviction scatters a session's blocks across the SSD and prevents large sequential reads. We present FlatCake, a flat KV storage architecture that exposes host DRAM and NVMe SSD as peer media in a unified 64-bit address space. Exploiting the fact that KV blocks are write-once, immutable, and reused at session scope, FlatCake places each block directly onto its target medium in session-contiguous, layer-major order at creation time and never migrates it. FlatCake streams coalesced SSD reads directly into a bounded GPU window via GPUDirect Storage and decouples DRAM and SSD request queues to prevent head-of-line blocking. On a production Claude Code trace with DeepSeek-V4-Flash, FlatCake sustains a 94% cache hit rate as load increases (versus 64% for Mooncake), eliminates Mooncake's $6.5\times$--$6.7\times$ SSD read amplification, and reduces peak-load mean TTFT by $1.6\times$ while improving total token throughput by $1.6\times$. Across 8K--128K contexts on Llama-3.1-70B, FlatCake further reduces mean TTFT by up to $2.4\times$ over Mooncake and $1.8\times$ over Mooncake-GDS.</p></details> | 10 figures |
| **[DeepInterestGR: Mining Deep Multi-Interest Using Multi-Modal LLMs for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18907v3)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce DeepInterestGR, a novel framework that integrates deep interest mining into the generative recommendation pipeline. This addresses the "Shallow Interest" problem: existing generative methods rely on surface-level textual features and fail to capture latent user motivations, limiting personalization depth and recommendation interpretability. Our approach leverages Multi-LLM Interest Mining (MLIM) via structured reasoning prompting, Reward-Labeled Deep Interest (RLDI) for quality control, and Interest-Enhanced Item Discretization (IEID) via RQ-VAE, combined with a two-stage SFT-GRPO training pipeline guided by an Interest-Aware Reward. We validate DeepInterestGR on three Amazon Review benchmarks (Beauty, Sports, Instruments), comparing against 14 state-of-the-art baselines including SASRec, BERT4Rec, TIGER, LC-Rec, and S-DPO. Our method achieves 12.4%-15.4% relative improvements on HR@10 and 15.5%-17.9% on NDCG@10, with cross-domain generalization gains of +24.8%. To our knowledge, this is the first work to demonstrate such significant gains by incorporating deep semantic interests into generative recommendation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can LLMs Faithfully Solve Ontology Engineering Tasks? A Mechanistic Interpretability Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38326v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ontological knowledge underpins large language model (LLM) performance on knowledge-intensive tasks. However, existing evaluations of LLMs' ontological understanding largely rely on behavioral correctness, leaving the underlying mechanisms unexplained. Existing mechanistic interpretability work has largely focused on instance-level factual knowledge, leaving ontological knowledge under-explored. To bridge this gap, we introduce MIOE, a causal-abstraction benchmark that evaluates interchange interventions for two ontology engineering tasks: entity typing and subsumption prediction. MIOE tests whether learned internal intervention targets can change an ontology-level target answer while preserving class-specific auxiliary information. Across Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B, we evaluate SAE-, DBM-, and DAS-based methods, including multi-task and multi-layer variants. Distributed subspace methods give the strongest results and generalize across prompt settings, but the induced changes remain only partially separated from auxiliary class-specific information. Multi-task objectives and contiguous layer-window interventions improve this balance, while class-level analyses reveal substantial variation across ontology classes. These results suggest that ontological predictions are mechanistically accessible in LLMs, although current methods do not yet isolate a cleanly disentangled causal mechanism for ontological knowledge.</p></details> |  |
| **[Arapai: An Offline-First LLM Architecture for Adaptive Learning in Low-Connectivity Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03339v10)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence and large language models (LLMs) are transforming educational technology by enabling conversational tutoring, personalised explanations, and inquiry-driven learning. However, most AI-based learning systems rely on continuous internet connectivity and cloud-based computation, limiting their use in bandwidth-constrained environments. This paper presents Arapai, an offline-first large language model architecture designed for AI-assisted learning in low-connectivity settings. The system performs all inference locally using quantized language models and incorporates hardware-aware model selection to enable deployment on low-specification, CPU-only devices. By removing dependence on cloud infrastructure, the system provides curriculum-aligned explanations and structured academic support through natural-language interaction. To support learners at different educational stages, the system includes adaptive response levels that generate explanations at varying levels of complexity: Simple English, Lower Secondary, Upper Secondary, and Technical. The system was evaluated with 120 students and 9 instructors from secondary and tertiary institutions under limited-connectivity conditions. Results indicate stable operation on legacy hardware, acceptable response times of 1-3 seconds for typical queries, and positive user perceptions of its effectiveness in supporting self-directed learning.</p></details> | <details><summary>The s...</summary><p>The statement that "The system was evaluated with 120 students and 9 instructors from secondary and tertiary institutions" is inaccurate</p></details> |
